The problem asks for a shortest command sequence to drive the credit counter $c$ negative, starting from mode $M_0$ with $c=0$.
Let's analyze the transitions and credit changes.
The rules are:
- M0: A (+2, M2), B (+2, M3), C (+1, M0)
- M1: A (-2, M0), B (0, M2), C (-1, M3)
- M2: A (+2, M0), B (+1, M3), C (+1, M2)
- M3: A (-1, M3), B (+1, M1), C (+1, M3)

We start at $(M_0, 0)$.
To make $c$ negative, we must eventually perform a command that decreases $c$. The commands that decrease $c$ are:
- A in M1 ($c \to c-2$)
- C in M1 ($c \to c-1$)
- A in M3 ($c \to c-1$)

Modes M0 and M2 do not have commands that decrease $c$.
Thus, we must enter M1 or M3.
From M0, the only way to enter M3 is via command B, which increases $c$ by 2.
From M0, entering M2 (via A) or staying in M0 (via C) increases $c$ and does not lead to a negative move.
So, the first command must be B to reach M3.
Path: $M_0 \xrightarrow{B} M_3$. State becomes $(M_3, 2)$.
Now we are in M3. We can use command A to decrease $c$ by 1.
Path: $M_3 \xrightarrow{A} M_3$. State becomes $(M_3, 1)$.
We can use A again.
Path: $M_3 \xrightarrow{A} M_3$. State becomes $(M_3, 0)$.
We can use A again.
Path: $M_3 \xrightarrow{A} M_3$. State becomes $(M_3, -1)$.
This sequence is B, A, A, A. It has length 4.
Let's check if a shorter sequence exists.
At step 1, we must be in M3 (via B) to have a chance to reduce $c$ (since M2 has no reduction and M0 has no reduction). The only other option is M1, but M1 is entered from M3. So we must go M0 -> M3 -> M1.
Sequence to M1: $M_0 \xrightarrow{B} M_3 \xrightarrow{B} M_1$. Credits: $0 \to 2 \to 3$.

The system has four modes $M_0, M_1, M_2, M_3$ and a credit counter $c$. We start at $(M_0, 0)$. We want to find the shortest sequence of commands from $\{A, B, C\}$ such that $c < 0$.

First, let's identify the transitions that decrease $c$:
- In mode $M_1$: $A \to c-2$, $C \to c-1$.
- In mode $M_3$: $A \to c-1$.
- In modes $M_0$ and $M_2$, all transitions increase $c$.

To decrease $c$, we must reach mode $M_1$ or $M_3$.
From $M_0$, the only way to enter $M_3$ is via $B$ ($c \to c+2$). The only way to enter $M_1$ is from $M_3$ via $B$ ($c \to c+1$).
So, to use a negative transition, we must pass through $M_3$.

Let's analyze the shortest sequences step by step.
**Start:** $(M_0, 0)$.

**Step 1:**
- $A \to (M_2, 2)$
- $B \to (M_3, 2)$
- $C \to (M_0, 1)$
The minimum credit is 1 (in $M_0$).

**Step 2:**
From $(M_2, 2)$: $A \to (M_0, 4)$, $B \to (M_3, 3)$, $C \to (M_2, 3)$.
From $(M_3, 2)$: $A \to (M_3, 1)$, $B \to (M_1, 3)$, $C \to (M_3, 3)$.
From $(M_0, 1)$: $A \to (M_2, 3)$, $B \to (M_3, 3)$, $C \to (M_0, 2)$.
The states reachable at Step 2 are $\{(M_0, 4), (M_3, 3), (M_2, 3), (M_3, 1), (M_1, 3), (M_0, 2)\}$.
The minimum credit is 1 (in $M_3$). No negative transitions can be applied yet to result in $c < 0$ (e.g., $1-1=0$, $3-1=2$, $3-2=1$).

**Step 3:**
From $(M_3, 1)$: $A \to (M_3, 0)$, $B \to (M_1, 2)$, $C \to (M_3, 2)$.
From $(M_1, 3)$: $A \to (M_0, 1)$, $B \to (M_2, 3)$, $C \to (M_3, 2)$.
From $(M_3, 3)$: $A \to (M_3, 2)$, $B \to (M_1, 4)$, $C \to (M_3, 4)$.
From others: $c$ increases or stays positive.
The states reachable at Step 3 with $c \le 0$ is only $\{(M_3, 0)\}$.
Since the minimum credit is 0, no step of length 3 can drive $c$ negative.

**Step 4:**
To drive $c$ negative, we must be in a state where a negative command can reduce $c$ below 0.
The only such state reachable at Step 3 is $(M_3, 0)$.
From $(M_3, 0)$, applying $A$ leads to $(M_3, -1)$.
To reach $(M_3, 0)$ at Step 3, we must follow the path:
1. Start $(M_0, 0) \xrightarrow{B} (M_3, 2)$.
2. $(M_3, 2) \xrightarrow{A} (M_3, 1)$.
3. $(M_3, 1) \xrightarrow{A} (M_3, 0)$.
4. $(M_3, 0) \xrightarrow{A} (M_3, -

The problem asks for a shortest command sequence starting from state $(M_0, c=0)$ that results in a negative credit counter value $c$. The command sequence consists of symbols from $\{A, B, C\}$. We need to determine the sequence and the credit value after each step.

Let's denote the state as $(M, c)$.
Initial state: $(M_0, 0)$.

We analyze the transitions and the effect on $c$ to find a path to a negative value.
To make $c$ negative, we must use commands that decrease $c$. These are:
- In $M_3$: command $A$ ($c \leftarrow c - 1$).
- In $M_1$: command $A$ ($c \leftarrow c - 2$) and command $C$ ($c \leftarrow c - 1$).

We start in $M_0$. The commands in $M_0$ all increase $c$: $A \to c+2$, $B \to c+2$, $C \to c+1$.
Thus, we must first transition to a mode where $c$ can decrease, i.e., $M_1$ or $M_3$.
- Transitions to $M_3$:
  - From $M_0$: $B$ ($c \leftarrow c+2$).
  - From $M_1$: $C$ ($c \leftarrow c-1$).
  - From $M_2$: $B$ ($c \leftarrow c+1$).
  - From $M_3$: $C$ ($c \leftarrow c+1$).
- Transitions to $M_1$:
  - From $M_3$: $B$ ($c \leftarrow c+1$).

Since we start at $c=0$, and entering $M_3$ from $M_0$ adds 2 credits, we reach $(M_3, 2)$ after command $B$.
From $(M_3, 2)$, we can use command $A$ to decrease $c$ by 1.
- $M_3(2) \xrightarrow{A} M_3(1)$.
- $M_3(1) \xrightarrow{A} M_3(0)$.
- $M_3(0) \xrightarrow{A} M_3(-1)$.

This sequence of commands is $B, A, A, A$.
Let's trace the credit values:
1. Start: $M_0, c=0$.
2. Command $B$: Transition $M_0 \to M_3$, $c \leftarrow 0 + 2 = 2$. State: $(M_3, 2)$.
3. Command $A$: Transition $M_3 \to M_3$, $c \leftarrow 2 - 1 = 1$. State: $(M_3, 1)$.
4. Command $A$: Transition $M_3 \to M_3$, $c \leftarrow 1 - 1 = 0$. State: $(M_3, 0)$.
5. Command $A$: Transition $M_3 \to M_3$, $c \leftarrow 0 - 1 = -1$. State: $(M_3, -1)$.

The credit value becomes negative (-1) after 4 steps.

One detail: "A fired event applies its effects in order."
Does this mean A is evaluated before n?
"Effects: A=false, n + 2, go DONE."
If this was relevant, the order would matter.
But here, effects are simple assignments and increments. Order usually doesn't matter for independent variables, unless one depends on the other.
Example: "A=true; if A then n=n+1".
But here effects are "A=false", "n + 2", "go DONE". These are independent.
Also, HELLO sets A=false.
If A was true, it becomes false.
Since A is never set to true, HELLO just sets A=false (no change).
So order doesn't matter.

Also, "Any other event is REJECTED: nothing changes and the event is counted."
Is it possible that rejected events change state in some other way? No, "nothing changes".

One small detail:
"An event fires only if the current state is in its allowed list AND its guard holds"
"guards are evaluated against register values just before the event"
"Effects in order"
"A fired event applies its effects in order"
"Any other event is REJECTED"

Is there any chance that "Effects in order" means something else?
Like `n+2` then `go FAULT` vs `go FAULT` then `n+2`?
Since `go FAULT` changes state, and other effects modify registers, it might matter if subsequent effects depended on the new state.
But effects of a single event are listed.
HELLO: A=false, n+2, go DONE.
Does `go DONE` affect `A=false`? No.
Does `A=false` affect `n+2`? No.
Does `n+2` affect `go DONE`? No.
Usually order is as written.
If order mattered for state change, it would be weird if state change happens in the middle of effects of the same event.
Usually state change is atomic or happens at the end.
But here it says "applies its effects in order".
For example, HELLO.
If A=false is first, then n+2, then go DONE.
If go DONE is last, then the state becomes DONE.
If go DONE was first, then A=false (in DONE?), n+2.
But effects are just assignments.
Since there is no interaction between effects (except potentially state-dependent effects within the same event, which is unlikely given the simple logic), the order is likely not an issue for correctness of the *next* event, but for the *current* event's effects if they depend on each other.
Since none of the effects depend on each other, the order is irrelevant.
So my simulation holds.

The only dependency is state-dependent allowed list check for *next* event.
Since my simulation updates state after effects, it is correct.
